Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really overwhelmed since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. Like, the art part is fine, mostly, but then there are invoices and client messages and all that stuff, and I just get so wound up about it that I don’t answer them.

And then it piles up, and I’ll be sitting at my kitchen table crying over something that should be simple. I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. It sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it’s been hard to manage.

Um… social gatherings? I don’t know. I mean, I’m not really a huge “go to every event” person anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s avoidance and what’s just me preferring to be home.

Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. It was after I took on this community arts contract, and around the same time I was trying to make the business feel more… official, I guess. Like more formal, not just me doing projects here and there.

Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard to separate because everything kind of gets tangled together. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.

And home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.

So yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.

I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. It’s like the second I think about invoices or replying to client messages, I get all wound up, and then I avoid it, which of course makes it worse. Then suddenly it’s not just the email, it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nAnd then I’ll forget some totally normal thing, like paying a bill, because I’m still stuck in my head about one client message. I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. Like an email I haven’t answered or whether I forgot some random errand.

So I don’t know if that counts as a change.\n\nBut no, nothing recent that’s stood out to me with appetite or weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or activities that are not related to your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard to separate because everything kind of gets tangled together. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.\n\nAnd home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. Like an email I haven’t answered or whether I forgot some random errand.\n\nI don’t know if I worry when there’s no reason. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into five other things, like I’ll look at the sink and remember I need dish soap, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether the grocery store changed their layout—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… maybe not “no reason,” exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself putting off daily chores or routines because they feel overwhelming?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"This has started affecting my freelance work and home responsibilities, like putting off a client reply and missing a utility payment while trying to keep up with the art display.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think so. Daily stuff has been getting pushed back because it feels like too much to switch gears, if that makes sense.

Like, I’ll be trying to keep up with the art display, and then I know I need to answer a client, but I’ll put it off because even opening the email feels like it’s going to turn into this whole thing. And then home things… I missed a utility payment recently, which was really stupid because it wasn’t that I couldn’t pay it, I just kept delaying it while I was focused on getting the display together.

So, yeah, chores and routine things are kind of sliding more than I’d like.

And then home things… I missed a utility payment recently, which was really stupid because it wasn’t that I couldn’t pay it, I just kept delaying it while I was focused on getting the display together.\n\nSo, yeah, chores and routine things are kind of sliding more than I’d like.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… habits or rituals… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of, like, I have to touch something a certain number of times or repeat something or else something bad happens.

I mean, I do kind of double-check things sometimes, but I think that’s just normal? Like looking for my keys before I leave, and then I get distracted because I’ll see a receipt in my bag and remember I meant to track an expense, and then I’m suddenly reorganizing my purse instead of leaving. Sorry—that’s probably not what you mean.

So no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual I feel compelled to do over and over.

I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. It’s like the second I think about invoices or replying to client messages, I get all wound up, and then I avoid it, which of course makes it worse. Then suddenly it’s not just the email, it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nAnd then I’ll forget some totally normal thing, like paying a bill, because I’m still stuck in my head about one client message. It’s frustrating, because I wanted the art to be the hard part, not… all the business stuff around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things other than just the business tasks related to your freelance work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I mostly notice it with the freelance stuff, like the messages and invoices. Other things… maybe I put things off, but I don’t know if it’s the same kind of avoiding.\n\nLike, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen instead, which is funny because I don’t even like cleaning the kitchen, but then I’ll end up reorganizing a drawer and somehow an hour is gone. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed avoiding other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings or events outside of your freelance work?\n\nPatient: Um… social gatherings? I don’t know. I mean, I’m not really a huge “go to every event” person anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s avoidance and what’s just me preferring to be home.\n\nLike if there’s a birthday thing or someone’s casual get-together, I might overthink whether I have the energy, but I don’t think it’s the same as the freelance stuff. With that, it feels more like… I can’t even open the message. Social stuff is more like, do I want to put on jeans and make small talk, you know?\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with avoiding social events, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Like… repeating behaviors as in checking things? I don’t think I have anything like that that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck thinking about something longer than I want to, but then I’m also the kind of person who will rewatch the same show while I sketch because it’s comforting, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any specific repeated behaviors or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or time when you started feeling overwhelmed with your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. Like more formal, not just me doing projects here and there.\n\nAt first I was kind of excited, honestly, because I like the arts stuff and I had all these ideas about making better forms and organizing things. I even bought these little folders—sorry, that’s not really the point. But maybe about a month after that, it started feeling like too much, and that’s when I noticed myself getting more overwhelmed and avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Has your overwhelm with the freelance business affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s affected them in a big obvious way, like nobody has sat me down or anything.\n\nI mean, I might be a little more distracted when I’m with people, or I’ll ramble about some invoice thing when my friend was probably just asking how my day was—actually I did that once at lunch and then realized I’d been talking about forms for like ten minutes. Sorry—that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual I feel compelled to do over and over.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often worry about everyday things, like finances or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do think about money or people in my life, but usually there’s some little reason for it, like a bill sitting on the counter or someone not texting back right away.

When things are actually going well… I guess I don’t really notice myself worrying just out of nowhere. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then remember something completely separate, like I was checking my bank app and then realized I hadn’t bought laundry detergent, and then I’m making a list instead of doing what I opened the app for.

So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s a constant thing when everything’s fine.

I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. It sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or fearful when thinking about your freelance business tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s like the second I think about invoices or replying to client messages, I get all wound up, and then I avoid it, which of course makes it worse. Then suddenly it’s not just the email, it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nAnd then I’ll forget some totally normal thing, like paying a bill, because I’m still stuck in my head about one client message. It’s frustrating, because I wanted the art to be the hard part, not… all the business stuff around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things other than just the business tasks related to your freelance work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I mostly notice it with the freelance stuff, like the messages and invoices. Other things… maybe I put things off, but I don’t know if it’s the same kind of avoiding.\n\nLike, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen instead, which is funny because I don’t even like cleaning the kitchen, but then I’ll end up reorganizing a drawer and somehow an hour is gone. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed avoiding other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings or events outside of your freelance work?\n\nPatient: Um… social gatherings? I don’t know. I mean, I’m not really a huge “go to every event” person anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s avoidance and what’s just me preferring to be home.\n\nLike if there’s a birthday thing or someone’s casual get-together, I might overthink whether I have the energy, but I don’t think it’s the same as the freelance stuff. With that, it feels more like… I can’t even open the message. Social stuff is more like, do I want to put on jeans and make small talk, you know?\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with avoiding social events, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Like… repeating behaviors as in checking things? I don’t think I have anything like that that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck thinking about something longer than I want to, but then I’m also the kind of person who will rewatch the same show while I sketch because it’s comforting, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any specific repeated behaviors or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or time when you started feeling overwhelmed with your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. It was after I took on this community arts contract, and around the same time I was trying to make the business feel more… official, I guess. Like more formal, not just me doing projects here and there.\n\nAt first I was kind of excited, honestly, because I like the arts stuff and I had all these ideas about making better forms and organizing things. I even bought these little folders—sorry, that’s not really the point. But maybe about a month after that, it started feeling like too much, and that’s when I noticed myself getting more overwhelmed and avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Has your overwhelm with the freelance business affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s affected them in a big obvious way, like nobody has sat me down or anything.\n\nI mean, I might be a little more distracted when I’m with people, or I’ll ramble about some invoice thing when my friend was probably just asking how my day was—actually I did that once at lunch and then realized I’d been talking about forms for like ten minutes. But I don’t know if that’s “affecting relationships” or just me being annoying.\n\nWith family, I guess I try not to bring it up too much because they’ll give advice, and then it turns into this whole thing. But I haven’t really noticed any major fallout or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I haven’t really noticed my weight changing, at least not in a way where my clothes fit differently or anything.\n\nAppetite-wise, I guess it’s kind of normal for me. Some days I’ll forget to eat lunch if I’m in the middle of something, but I’ve always been a little like that, especially if I’m doing art stuff and get caught up mixing colors or looking for the right reference photo. So I don’t know if that counts as a change.\n\nBut no, nothing recent that’s stood out to me with appetite or weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or activities that are not related to your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard to separate because everything kind of gets tangled together. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.\n\nAnd home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off daily chores or routines because they feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Daily stuff has been getting pushed back because it feels like too much to switch gears, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to keep up with the art display, and then I know I need to answer a client, but I’ll put it off because even opening the email feels like it’s going to turn into this whole thing. And then home things… I missed a utility payment recently, which was really stupid because it wasn’t that I couldn’t pay it, I just kept delaying it while I was focused on getting the display together.\n\nSo, yeah, chores and routine things are kind of sliding more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… habits or rituals… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of, like, I have to touch something a certain number of times or repeat something or else something bad happens.\n\nI mean, I do kind of double-check things sometimes, but I think that’s just normal? Like looking for my keys before I leave, and then I get distracted because I’ll see a receipt in my bag and remember I meant to track an expense, and then I’m suddenly reorganizing my purse instead of leaving. Sorry—that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual I feel compelled to do over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like finances or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do think about money or people in my life, but usually there’s some little reason for it, like a bill sitting on the counter or someone not texting back right away.\n\nWhen things are actually going well… I guess I don’t really notice myself worrying just out of nowhere. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then remember something completely separate, like I was checking my bank app and then realized I hadn’t bought laundry detergent, and then I’m making a list instead of doing what I opened the app for.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s a constant thing when everything’s fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you frequently worry about ordinary, everyday things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it frequent, no. I mean, I do worry about normal things sometimes—like if there’s a bill or if I forgot to reply to someone—but I don’t think I’m sitting around worrying about everything all the time.

It’s more like… something pops up and then my mind follows it somewhere else. Like I’ll think about groceries and then remember I never cleaned out the fridge, and then I’m thinking about whether I should reorganize the kitchen, which is not really the question, sorry.

So… everyday worries happen, but I wouldn’t say it’s constant or for no reason.

I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. Then suddenly it’s not just the email, it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nAnd then I’ll forget some totally normal thing, like paying a bill, because I’m still stuck in my head about one client message. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed avoiding other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings or events outside of your freelance work?\n\nPatient: Um… social gatherings? I don’t know. I mean, I’m not really a huge “go to every event” person anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s avoidance and what’s just me preferring to be home.\n\nLike if there’s a birthday thing or someone’s casual get-together, I might overthink whether I have the energy, but I don’t think it’s the same as the freelance stuff. With that, it feels more like… I can’t even open the message. Social stuff is more like, do I want to put on jeans and make small talk, you know?\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with avoiding social events, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have anything like that that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck thinking about something longer than I want to, but then I’m also the kind of person who will rewatch the same show while I sketch because it’s comforting, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any specific repeated behaviors or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or time when you started feeling overwhelmed with your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. I even bought these little folders—sorry, that’s not really the point. I don’t think it’s affected them in a big obvious way, like nobody has sat me down or anything.\n\nI mean, I might be a little more distracted when I’m with people, or I’ll ramble about some invoice thing when my friend was probably just asking how my day was—actually I did that once at lunch and then realized I’d been talking about forms for like ten minutes. I mean, I do worry about normal things sometimes—like if there’s a bill or if I forgot to reply to someone—but I don’t think I’m sitting around worrying about everything all the time.\n\nIt’s more like… something pops up and then my mind follows it somewhere else. Like I’ll think about groceries and then remember I never cleaned out the fridge, and then I’m thinking about whether I should reorganize the kitchen, which is not really the question, sorry.\n\nSo… everyday worries happen, but I wouldn’t say it’s constant or for no reason.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your avoidance of tasks is mainly because they make you anxious or because you just don't feel like doing them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… it’s more because they make me anxious. Not every task, like I can wash dishes or whatever, though sometimes I’ll start doing that instead of the thing I’m supposed to be doing.

The stuff I avoid is mostly the freelance business stuff. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual business, invoices and client messages make me get really wound up. And then I put them off, and it snowballs until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying over an email or I realize I forgot something normal, like paying a bill. So… I don’t think it’s just that I don’t feel like doing it. It feels more like I can’t make myself open it because I’m already tense about it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really overwhelmed since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. Like, the art part is fine, mostly, but then there are invoices and client messages and all that stuff, and I just get so wound up about it that I don’t answer them.\n\nAnd then it piles up, and I’ll be sitting at my kitchen table crying over something that should be simple. I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. It sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or fearful when thinking about your freelance business tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s like the second I think about invoices or replying to client messages, I get all wound up, and then I avoid it, which of course makes it worse. Then suddenly it’s not just the email, it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nAnd then I’ll forget some totally normal thing, like paying a bill, because I’m still stuck in my head about one client message. It’s frustrating, because I wanted the art to be the hard part, not… all the business stuff around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things other than just the business tasks related to your freelance work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I mostly notice it with the freelance stuff, like the messages and invoices. Other things… maybe I put things off, but I don’t know if it’s the same kind of avoiding.\n\nLike, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen instead, which is funny because I don’t even like cleaning the kitchen, but then I’ll end up reorganizing a drawer and somehow an hour is gone. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed avoiding other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings or events outside of your freelance work?\n\nPatient: Um… social gatherings? I don’t know. I mean, I’m not really a huge “go to every event” person anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s avoidance and what’s just me preferring to be home.\n\nLike if there’s a birthday thing or someone’s casual get-together, I might overthink whether I have the energy, but I don’t think it’s the same as the freelance stuff. With that, it feels more like… I can’t even open the message. Social stuff is more like, do I want to put on jeans and make small talk, you know?\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with avoiding social events, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Like… repeating behaviors as in checking things? I don’t think I have anything like that that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck thinking about something longer than I want to, but then I’m also the kind of person who will rewatch the same show while I sketch because it’s comforting, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any specific repeated behaviors or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or time when you started feeling overwhelmed with your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. It was after I took on this community arts contract, and around the same time I was trying to make the business feel more… official, I guess. Like more formal, not just me doing projects here and there.\n\nAt first I was kind of excited, honestly, because I like the arts stuff and I had all these ideas about making better forms and organizing things. I even bought these little folders—sorry, that’s not really the point. But maybe about a month after that, it started feeling like too much, and that’s when I noticed myself getting more overwhelmed and avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Has your overwhelm with the freelance business affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s affected them in a big obvious way, like nobody has sat me down or anything.\n\nI mean, I might be a little more distracted when I’m with people, or I’ll ramble about some invoice thing when my friend was probably just asking how my day was—actually I did that once at lunch and then realized I’d been talking about forms for like ten minutes. But I don’t know if that’s “affecting relationships” or just me being annoying.\n\nWith family, I guess I try not to bring it up too much because they’ll give advice, and then it turns into this whole thing. But I haven’t really noticed any major fallout or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I haven’t really noticed my weight changing, at least not in a way where my clothes fit differently or anything.\n\nAppetite-wise, I guess it’s kind of normal for me. Some days I’ll forget to eat lunch if I’m in the middle of something, but I’ve always been a little like that, especially if I’m doing art stuff and get caught up mixing colors or looking for the right reference photo. So I don’t know if that counts as a change.\n\nBut no, nothing recent that’s stood out to me with appetite or weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or activities that are not related to your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard to separate because everything kind of gets tangled together. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.\n\nAnd home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. Like an email I haven’t answered or whether I forgot some random errand.\n\nI don’t know if I worry when there’s no reason. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into five other things, like I’ll look at the sink and remember I need dish soap, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether the grocery store changed their layout—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… maybe not “no reason,” exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off daily chores or routines because they feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Daily stuff has been getting pushed back because it feels like too much to switch gears, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to keep up with the art display, and then I know I need to answer a client, but I’ll put it off because even opening the email feels like it’s going to turn into this whole thing. I think if they didn’t make me anxious, I probably wouldn’t avoid them as much. Like, I don’t avoid everything—if it’s something simple, I can usually just do it.

It’s really been since I tried to turn my art into an actual freelance business. So no, I don’t think I’d avoid it the same way if it didn’t make me feel like that.

I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. Then suddenly it’s not just the email, it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nAnd then I’ll forget some totally normal thing, like paying a bill, because I’m still stuck in my head about one client message. I mean, I mostly notice it with the freelance stuff, like the messages and invoices. Like… repeating behaviors as in checking things? I don’t think I have anything like that that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck thinking about something longer than I want to, but then I’m also the kind of person who will rewatch the same show while I sketch because it’s comforting, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any specific repeated behaviors or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or time when you started feeling overwhelmed with your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. I even bought these little folders—sorry, that’s not really the point. But maybe about a month after that, it started feeling like too much, and that’s when I noticed myself getting more overwhelmed and avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Has your overwhelm with the freelance business affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s affected them in a big obvious way, like nobody has sat me down or anything.\n\nI mean, I might be a little more distracted when I’m with people, or I’ll ramble about some invoice thing when my friend was probably just asking how my day was—actually I did that once at lunch and then realized I’d been talking about forms for like ten minutes. So I don’t know if that counts as a change.\n\nBut no, nothing recent that’s stood out to me with appetite or weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or activities that are not related to your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard to separate because everything kind of gets tangled together. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.\n\nAnd home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. I haven’t really noticed my weight changing, at least not in a way where my clothes fit differently or anything.\n\nAppetite-wise, I guess it’s kind of normal for me. Some days I’ll forget to eat lunch if I’m in the middle of something, but I’ve always been a little like that, especially if I’m doing art stuff and get caught up mixing colors or looking for the right reference photo. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.\n\nAnd home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. No, I don’t think so. I mean, I might still procrastinate a little—everybody does that, right? Like I’ll put off laundry until it’s ridiculous, and then suddenly I’m sorting socks at midnight, which is… not really the same thing.

Not really as much, no. I mean, I get stressed about normal things sometimes—like anyone does—but the thing that really sets me off is the freelance business stuff.

Like… repeating behaviors as in checking things? I don’t think I have anything like that that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck thinking about something longer than I want to, but then I’m also the kind of person who will rewatch the same show while I sketch because it’s comforting, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any specific repeated behaviors or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or time when you started feeling overwhelmed with your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. It was after I took on this community arts contract, and around the same time I was trying to make the business feel more… official, I guess. Like more formal, not just me doing projects here and there.\n\nAt first I was kind of excited, honestly, because I like the arts stuff and I had all these ideas about making better forms and organizing things. I even bought these little folders—sorry, that’s not really the point. But maybe about a month after that, it started feeling like too much, and that’s when I noticed myself getting more overwhelmed and avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Has your overwhelm with the freelance business affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s affected them in a big obvious way, like nobody has sat me down or anything.\n\nI mean, I might be a little more distracted when I’m with people, or I’ll ramble about some invoice thing when my friend was probably just asking how my day was—actually I did that once at lunch and then realized I’d been talking about forms for like ten minutes. But I don’t know if that’s “affecting relationships” or just me being annoying.\n\nWith family, I guess I try not to bring it up too much because they’ll give advice, and then it turns into this whole thing. But I haven’t really noticed any major fallout or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I haven’t really noticed my weight changing, at least not in a way where my clothes fit differently or anything.\n\nAppetite-wise, I guess it’s kind of normal for me. Some days I’ll forget to eat lunch if I’m in the middle of something, but I’ve always been a little like that, especially if I’m doing art stuff and get caught up mixing colors or looking for the right reference photo. So I don’t know if that counts as a change.\n\nBut no, nothing recent that’s stood out to me with appetite or weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or activities that are not related to your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard to separate because everything kind of gets tangled together. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.\n\nAnd home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. Like an email I haven’t answered or whether I forgot some random errand.\n\nI don’t know if I worry when there’s no reason. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into five other things, like I’ll look at the sink and remember I need dish soap, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether the grocery store changed their layout—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… maybe not “no reason,” exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off daily chores or routines because they feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Daily stuff has been getting pushed back because it feels like too much to switch gears, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to keep up with the art display, and then I know I need to answer a client, but I’ll put it off because even opening the email feels like it’s going to turn into this whole thing. And then home things… I missed a utility payment recently, which was really stupid because it wasn’t that I couldn’t pay it, I just kept delaying it while I was focused on getting the display together.\n\nSo, yeah, chores and routine things are kind of sliding more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… habits or rituals… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of, like, I have to touch something a certain number of times or repeat something or else something bad happens.\n\nI mean, I do kind of double-check things sometimes, but I think that’s just normal? Like looking for my keys before I leave, and then I get distracted because I’ll see a receipt in my bag and remember I meant to track an expense, and then I’m suddenly reorganizing my purse instead of leaving. Sorry—that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual I feel compelled to do over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like finances or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do think about money or people in my life, but usually there’s some little reason for it, like a bill sitting on the counter or someone not texting back right away.\n\nWhen things are actually going well… I guess I don’t really notice myself worrying just out of nowhere. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then remember something completely separate, like I was checking my bank app and then realized I hadn’t bought laundry detergent, and then I’m making a list instead of doing what I opened the app for.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s a constant thing when everything’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently worry about ordinary, everyday things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it frequent, no. I mean, I do worry about normal things sometimes—like if there’s a bill or if I forgot to reply to someone—but I don’t think I’m sitting around worrying about everything all the time.\n\nIt’s more like… something pops up and then my mind follows it somewhere else. Like I’ll think about groceries and then remember I never cleaned out the fridge, and then I’m thinking about whether I should reorganize the kitchen, which is not really the question, sorry.\n\nSo… everyday worries happen, but I wouldn’t say it’s constant or for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of tasks is mainly because they make you anxious or because you just don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s more because they make me anxious. Not every task, like I can wash dishes or whatever, though sometimes I’ll start doing that instead of the thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nThe stuff I avoid is mostly the freelance business stuff. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual business, invoices and client messages make me get really wound up. And then I put them off, and it snowballs until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying over an email or I realize I forgot something normal, like paying a bill. So… I don’t think it’s just that I don’t feel like doing it. It feels more like I can’t make myself open it because I’m already tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid these tasks if they didn't make you anxious?\n\nPatient: I think if they didn’t make me anxious, I probably wouldn’t avoid them as much. Like, I don’t avoid everything—if it’s something simple, I can usually just do it.\n\nIt’s really been since I tried to turn my art into an actual freelance business. The invoices, client messages, all that stuff… I get so wound up about it, and then I put it off, and then it becomes this whole thing. I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table crying over an email, or I’ll realize I forgot to pay a normal bill because I was avoiding the business stuff.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’d avoid it the same way if it didn’t make me feel like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still put off tasks like invoices and client messages if you didn't feel anxious about them?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I might still procrastinate a little—everybody does that, right? Like I’ll put off laundry until it’s ridiculous, and then suddenly I’m sorting socks at midnight, which is… not really the same thing.\n\nWith the invoices and client messages, it’s different. Ever since I tried to make my art into a real freelance business, those things just make me get so wound up. Then I avoid them, and it builds up until I’m crying at my kitchen table over something that should just be a normal email, or I forget regular stuff like paying a bill. So if I didn’t feel anxious about them, I don’t think I’d put them off like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about things outside of your freelance business as much as you do about the business tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really as much, no. I mean, I get stressed about normal things sometimes—like anyone does—but the thing that really sets me off is the freelance business stuff.\n\nIt’s like, ever since I tried to make my art into an actual business, invoices and client messages just… I don’t know, they make me feel wound up right away. Then I avoid them, and then it becomes this whole mess where I’m crying at the kitchen table over an email. Or I get so stuck on avoiding that stuff that I forget normal things, like paying a bill.\n\nI guess outside of that, it’s not the same level. The business tasks are the main thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s that they make it feel like my art isn’t just my art anymore, it’s like… a real business, with expectations and deadlines and people waiting on me. And then I start worrying I’m doing it wrong.

Like, I came in because ever since I tried to turn my art into a real freelance thing, those invoices and client messages just get me so wound up. I’ll avoid them, and then it piles up, and suddenly I’m crying at the kitchen table over an email that probably isn’t even that big of a deal.

And then the stupid part is I’ll forget normal stuff too, like paying a bill, because I’m stuck avoiding the business stuff. I don’t know if it’s the invoice itself or just what it represents, I guess.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really overwhelmed since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. Like, the art part is fine, mostly, but then there are invoices and client messages and all that stuff, and I just get so wound up about it that I don’t answer them.\n\nAnd then it piles up, and I’ll be sitting at my kitchen table crying over something that should be simple. I even forget normal things, like paying a bill, because my brain is stuck on some client email. It sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or fearful when thinking about your freelance business tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s like the second I think about invoices or replying to client messages, I get all wound up, and then I avoid it, which of course makes it worse. Then suddenly it’s not just the email, it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nAnd then I’ll forget some totally normal thing, like paying a bill, because I’m still stuck in my head about one client message. It’s frustrating, because I wanted the art to be the hard part, not… all the business stuff around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things other than just the business tasks related to your freelance work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I mostly notice it with the freelance stuff, like the messages and invoices. Other things… maybe I put things off, but I don’t know if it’s the same kind of avoiding.\n\nLike, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen instead, which is funny because I don’t even like cleaning the kitchen, but then I’ll end up reorganizing a drawer and somehow an hour is gone. So… I guess I haven’t really noticed avoiding other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings or events outside of your freelance work?\n\nPatient: Um… social gatherings? I don’t know. I mean, I’m not really a huge “go to every event” person anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s avoidance and what’s just me preferring to be home.\n\nLike if there’s a birthday thing or someone’s casual get-together, I might overthink whether I have the energy, but I don’t think it’s the same as the freelance stuff. With that, it feels more like… I can’t even open the message. Social stuff is more like, do I want to put on jeans and make small talk, you know?\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with avoiding social events, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Like… repeating behaviors as in checking things? I don’t think I have anything like that that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck thinking about something longer than I want to, but then I’m also the kind of person who will rewatch the same show while I sketch because it’s comforting, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nI guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any specific repeated behaviors or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific event or time when you started feeling overwhelmed with your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think there was a point where it kind of shifted. It was after I took on this community arts contract, and around the same time I was trying to make the business feel more… official, I guess. Like more formal, not just me doing projects here and there.\n\nAt first I was kind of excited, honestly, because I like the arts stuff and I had all these ideas about making better forms and organizing things. I even bought these little folders—sorry, that’s not really the point. But maybe about a month after that, it started feeling like too much, and that’s when I noticed myself getting more overwhelmed and avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Has your overwhelm with the freelance business affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think it’s affected them in a big obvious way, like nobody has sat me down or anything.\n\nI mean, I might be a little more distracted when I’m with people, or I’ll ramble about some invoice thing when my friend was probably just asking how my day was—actually I did that once at lunch and then realized I’d been talking about forms for like ten minutes. But I don’t know if that’s “affecting relationships” or just me being annoying.\n\nWith family, I guess I try not to bring it up too much because they’ll give advice, and then it turns into this whole thing. But I haven’t really noticed any major fallout or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I haven’t really noticed my weight changing, at least not in a way where my clothes fit differently or anything.\n\nAppetite-wise, I guess it’s kind of normal for me. Some days I’ll forget to eat lunch if I’m in the middle of something, but I’ve always been a little like that, especially if I’m doing art stuff and get caught up mixing colors or looking for the right reference photo. So I don’t know if that counts as a change.\n\nBut no, nothing recent that’s stood out to me with appetite or weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks or activities that are not related to your freelance business?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard to separate because everything kind of gets tangled together. Like I’ll tell myself I’m only focusing on the art display because that’s urgent, and then suddenly I’ve put off replying to a client email that I really should have answered.\n\nAnd home stuff too, which is embarrassing. I missed a utility payment recently—not because I didn’t have the money, just because I kept thinking, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then I was comparing where to hang pieces and making labels and… I don’t know, suddenly it was overdue.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am avoiding other tasks, or at least letting them slide while I’m trying to keep up with the freelance stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry, but usually there’s something attached to it, even if it’s kind of small. Like an email I haven’t answered or whether I forgot some random errand.\n\nI don’t know if I worry when there’s no reason. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into five other things, like I’ll look at the sink and remember I need dish soap, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether the grocery store changed their layout—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… maybe not “no reason,” exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off daily chores or routines because they feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Daily stuff has been getting pushed back because it feels like too much to switch gears, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to keep up with the art display, and then I know I need to answer a client, but I’ll put it off because even opening the email feels like it’s going to turn into this whole thing. And then home things… I missed a utility payment recently, which was really stupid because it wasn’t that I couldn’t pay it, I just kept delaying it while I was focused on getting the display together.\n\nSo, yeah, chores and routine things are kind of sliding more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… habits or rituals… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of, like, I have to touch something a certain number of times or repeat something or else something bad happens.\n\nI mean, I do kind of double-check things sometimes, but I think that’s just normal? Like looking for my keys before I leave, and then I get distracted because I’ll see a receipt in my bag and remember I meant to track an expense, and then I’m suddenly reorganizing my purse instead of leaving. Sorry—that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual I feel compelled to do over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like finances or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I do think about money or people in my life, but usually there’s some little reason for it, like a bill sitting on the counter or someone not texting back right away.\n\nWhen things are actually going well… I guess I don’t really notice myself worrying just out of nowhere. Sometimes I’ll start thinking about one thing and then remember something completely separate, like I was checking my bank app and then realized I hadn’t bought laundry detergent, and then I’m making a list instead of doing what I opened the app for.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s a constant thing when everything’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently worry about ordinary, everyday things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it frequent, no. I mean, I do worry about normal things sometimes—like if there’s a bill or if I forgot to reply to someone—but I don’t think I’m sitting around worrying about everything all the time.\n\nIt’s more like… something pops up and then my mind follows it somewhere else. Like I’ll think about groceries and then remember I never cleaned out the fridge, and then I’m thinking about whether I should reorganize the kitchen, which is not really the question, sorry.\n\nSo… everyday worries happen, but I wouldn’t say it’s constant or for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of tasks is mainly because they make you anxious or because you just don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s more because they make me anxious. Not every task, like I can wash dishes or whatever, though sometimes I’ll start doing that instead of the thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nThe stuff I avoid is mostly the freelance business stuff. I came in because ever since I tried to make my art into an actual business, invoices and client messages make me get really wound up. And then I put them off, and it snowballs until I’m sitting at my kitchen table crying over an email or I realize I forgot something normal, like paying a bill. So… I don’t think it’s just that I don’t feel like doing it. It feels more like I can’t make myself open it because I’m already tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid these tasks if they didn't make you anxious?\n\nPatient: I think if they didn’t make me anxious, I probably wouldn’t avoid them as much.
